Moreover, the risk functionals presented in Table
1
satisfy Assumption
2.2
, that is, the risk functionals is OT-continuity under that the cost function
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=||y_{1}-y_{2}||^{q}_{\mathcal{Y}}
for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
with
q\in[1,\infty)
. We present the result in the following lemma.

Lemma 9
.

The risk functionals presented in Table
1
is OT-continuity under that the cost function
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=||y_{1}-y_{2}||^{q}_{\mathcal{Y}}
for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
with
q\in[1,\infty)
.

3.1
Conditional Expectation

We begin with the base case of the conditional expectation problem under its union-ball formulation:

\displaystyle\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sup_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Y}\in\bigcup\limits_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}}\mathbb{B}_{\delta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}\right)}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Y}}\left[\ell(Y^{\top}\alpha)\right]=\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sup_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}}\left\{\sup_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Y}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}\right)}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Y}}\left[\ell(Y^{\top}\alpha)\right]\right\}.

(15)

Invoking the regularization reformulation of the inner worst-case subproblem (
Wu et al. 2022
, see Section EC.2), we obtain a family of robust optimization problems. As we show below, these problems admit convex reformulations.

Proposition 10
.

(i)

Let
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=||y_{1}-y_{2}||_{\mathcal{Y}}
for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
and
\ell:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a convex and Lipschitz continuous function.
The problem (
15
) is equivalent to the robust optimization problem

\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sup_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{+}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\ell(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha)+\operatorname{Lip}(\ell)\delta||\alpha||_{*},

and can be solved by the convex program

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},v}~\sigma^{*}(v\mid\mathcal{V}_{+})

\displaystyle~~~\text{s.t.}~v_{i}\geqslant\ell(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha),~i=1,...,N,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~v_{N+1}\geqslant\operatorname{Lip}(\ell)||\alpha||_{*}.

(ii)

Let
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=||y_{1}-y_{2}||_{\mathcal{Y}}^{q}
for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
and
q\in(1,\infty)
.

(1)

Assume the loss function
\ell
takes one of the following two forms, multiplied by
C>0
:

(a)

\ell_{1}(x)=x+b
or
\ell_{1}(x)=-x+b
with some
b\in\mathbb{R}
;

(b)

\ell_{2}(x)=\left|x-b_{1}\right|+b_{2}
with some
b_{1},b_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
.

The problem (
15
) is equivalent to the robust optimization problem

\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sup_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{+}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\ell(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha)+C\delta^{\frac{1}{q}}||\alpha||_{*},

and can be solved by the convex program

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},v}~\sigma^{*}(v|\mathcal{V}_{+})+C_{q}v_{N+1}\left\|\frac{\alpha}{v_{N+1}}\right\|_{*}^{\frac{q}{q-1}}

\displaystyle~~~\text{s.t.}~v_{i}\geqslant\ell(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha),~i=1,...,N,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~v_{N+1}\geqslant 0,

where
C_{q}=C^{\frac{q}{q-1}}\left(q^{\frac{1}{1-q}}-q^{\frac{q}{1-q}}\right)
.

(2)

Assume the loss function
\ell
is of the form
\ell(x)=\left(C\cdot\ell_{i}(x)\right)^{q}
, where
C>0
,
q\in(1,\infty)
, and
\ell_{i}
is one of the following:

(a)

\ell_{1}(x)=(x-b)_{+}
with some
b\in\mathbb{R}
;

(b)

\ell_{2}(x)=(x-b)_{-}
with some
b\in\mathbb{R}
;

(c)

\ell_{3}(x)=\left(\left|x-b_{1}\right|-b_{2}\right)_{+}
with some
b_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
b_{2}\geqslant 0
;

(d)

\ell_{4}(x)=\left|x-b_{1}\right|+b_{2}
with some
b_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
b_{2}>0
.

The problem (
15
) is equivalent to the robust optimization problem

\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sup_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{+}}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\ell(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha)\right)^{1/q}+C\delta^{\frac{1}{q}}||\alpha||_{*}\right]^{q},

and can be solved by the convex program

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},v,y\geqslant 0}\left[\sigma^{*}(v|\mathcal{V}_{+})+C_{q}^{1}y+C_{q}v_{N+1}\left\|\frac{\alpha}{v_{N+1}}\right\|_{*}^{\frac{q}{q-1}}\right]^{q}

\displaystyle~~~~~~\text{s.t.}~v_{i}\geqslant\frac{\ell(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha)}{y^{q-1}},~i=1,...,N,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~v_{N+1}\geqslant 0.

where
C_{q}^{1}=q^{\frac{1}{1-q}}-q^{\frac{q}{1-q}}
and
C_{q}=C^{\frac{q}{q-1}}\left(q^{\frac{1}{1-q}}-q^{\frac{q}{1-q}}\right)
.

More generally, even without regularization equivalents, the decomposition remains powerful: by isolating each Wasserstein DRO subproblem, it enables us to exploit their projection property
(
Wu et al. 2022
)
:

\displaystyle\sup_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Y}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}\right)}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Y}}\left[\ell(Y^{\top}\alpha)\right]=\sup_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Z}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta\|\alpha\|_{*}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha}\right)}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Z}}\left[\ell(Z)\right],

which yields a one-dimensional reformulation that is considerably easier to handle. Leveraging this insight, we derive a final convex program reformulation of the conditional expectation problem (
15
), applicable to any suitably regular convex loss function
\ell
.

Proposition 11
.

Let
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=||y_{1}-y_{2}||_{\mathcal{Y}}^{q}
for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
and
q\in(1,\infty)
. Assuming the loss function
\ell
is convex and satisfies
\ell(x)-\ell(x_{0})\leqslant L|x-x_{0}|^{q}+M,x\in\mathbb{R}
for some
L,M>0
, and some
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, the problem (
15
) can be solved by the convex program

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},v,\eta\geqslant 0}~\sigma^{*}(v|\mathcal{V}_{+})

\displaystyle~~~~~~\text{s.t.}~v_{i}\geqslant\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{z\cdot\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha-\ell^{*}(z)+\eta\cdot\frac{|z|^{q^{*}}}{q^{*}q^{q^{*}-1}}\right\},~i=1,...,N,

(16)

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~v_{N+1}\geqslant||\alpha||_{*}^{q}/\eta^{q-1}.

Constraints like (
16
), where the right-hand side involves an optimization problem, commonly arise in dual reformulations of Wasserstein DRO. What sets (
16
) apart is that the optimization is univariate, which not only simplifies analysis and computation but also enables further problem-specific reductions, as we demonstrate below.

Example 3.1
.

(i)

(Piecewise linear convex loss function) Let
\ell(x)=\sup_{j=1,...,k}a_{j}x+b_{j}
. The convex conjugate function is

\ell^{*}(z)=\begin{cases}-\max_{j:a_{j}=z}b_{j}&\text{ if }z\in\left\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\right\},\\
+\infty&\text{ otherwise }.\end{cases}

Without loss of generality, assume that
a_{j_{1}}\neq a_{j_{2}}
for
{j_{1}}\neq{j_{2}}
. Thus, for
q>1
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{zt-\ell^{*}(z)+\eta\cdot\frac{|z|^{q^{*}}}{q^{*}q^{q^{*}-1}}\right\}=\max_{j=1,\ldots,k}\left\{a_{j}t+b_{j}+\eta\cdot\frac{|a_{j}|^{q^{*}}}{q^{*}q^{q^{*}-1}}\right\}.

(ii)

(Piecewise quadratic convex loss function) Let
\ell(x)=\sup_{j=1,...,k}a_{j}x^{2}+b_{j}x+c_{j}
and
a_{j}>0
for
j=1,...,k
. The convex conjugate function is

\ell^{*}(z)=\min_{j=1,\ldots,k}\left\{\frac{\left(z-b_{j}\right)^{2}}{4a_{j}}-c_{j}\right\}.

Thus, for
q=2
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{zt-\ell^{*}(z)+\eta\cdot\frac{|z|^{q^{*}}}{q^{*}q^{q^{*}-1}}\right\}=\max_{j=1,\ldots,k}\left\{\frac{a_{j}t^{2}+b_{j}t}{1-a_{j}\eta}+c_{j}+\frac{a_{j}b_{j}^{2}\eta}{4\left(1-a_{j}\eta\right)}\right\},

and
\eta<\min_{j=1,...,k}\frac{1}{a_{j}}
.

(iii)

(Power loss function) Let
\ell(x)=\frac{|x|^{q}}{q}
for
q>1
. The convex conjugate function is

\ell^{*}(z)=\frac{|z|^{q^{*}}}{q^{*}}.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{zt-\ell^{*}(z)+\eta\cdot\frac{|z|^{q^{*}}}{q^{*}q^{q^{*}-1}}\right\}=\frac{|t|^{q}}{q}\left(1-\frac{\eta}{q^{q^{*}-1}}\right)^{-(q-1)},

and
\eta<q^{q^{*}-1}
.

Thus far, our tractable reformulations have been kept general, applicable to any distributionally robust CRM that admits a union-ball representation with a convex admissible set
\mathcal{V}
. To illustrate how the support function
\sigma^{*}(v|\mathcal{V}_{+})
is derived, we now apply the admissible sets from Theorems
3
and
6
. These examples also enable direct comparison between the final formulations from our union-ball approach and those in existing OT-based methods. Recall that
\mathcal{N}_{\omega}=[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]\subset(0,1]
.

Example 3.2
.

Denote
p^{\prime}=(p,\delta,\varepsilon)
. Following Theorem
3
, the conjugate of the indicator function of
\mathcal{V}_{+}
is given by

\displaystyle\sigma^{*}(v\mid\mathcal{V}_{+})=\sup_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{+}}v^{\top}(p,\delta)=\begin{cases}\sup v^{\top}(p,\delta)+0\cdot\varepsilon\\
\text{s.t. }Ap^{\prime}\leqslant b,\;p^{\prime}\geqslant 0\end{cases}=\begin{cases}\inf z^{\top}b\\
\text{s.t. }A^{\top}z\geqslant(v,0),\;z\geqslant 0,\end{cases}

where
A\in\mathbb{R}^{(N+6)\times(N+2)}
,and
b\in\mathbb{R}^{N+6}
are defined by

\displaystyle A=\begin{pmatrix}1&\dots&1&0&0\\
-1&\dots&-1&0&0\\
0&\dots&0&0&1\\
0&\dots&0&0&-1\\
1&\dots&0&0&-\frac{1}{N}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&\dots&1&0&-\frac{1}{N}\\
-a_{1}&\dots&-a_{N}&-a_{N+1}&-a_{N+2}\\
a_{1}&\dots&a_{N}&a_{N+1}&a_{N+2}\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{ with }a=\begin{pmatrix}-d_{1}\\
\vdots\\
-d_{m}\\
d_{m+1}\\
\vdots\\
d_{N}\\
-1\\[2.15277pt]
\displaystyle\delta_{0}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}d_{i}\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{ and }b=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-1\\[3.0pt]
\tfrac{1}{\omega_{1}}\\
-\tfrac{1}{\omega_{2}}\\[3.0pt]
0\\
\vdots\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

Example 3.3
.

Note that
\mathcal{N}_{\omega}=[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]\subset(0,1]
. Then
{\varepsilon}=\min_{\beta\in\mathcal{N}_{\omega}}\beta=\omega_{1}
. Following Theorem
6
, the conjugate of the indicator function of
\mathcal{V}_{+}
is given by

\displaystyle\sigma^{*}(v\mid\mathcal{V}_{+})=\sup_{p^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{+}}v^{\top}p^{\prime}=\begin{cases}\sup v^{\top}p^{\prime}\\
\text{s.t.}~A^{\prime}p^{\prime}\leqslant b^{\prime},~p^{\prime}\geqslant 0\end{cases}=\begin{cases}\inf z^{\top}b^{\prime}\\
\text{s.t.}~(A^{\prime})^{\top}z\geqslant v,~z\geqslant 0,\end{cases}

where
A^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{(N+4)\times(N+1)}
, and
b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{N+4}
are defined by

\displaystyle A^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\dots&1&0\\
-1&\dots&-1&0\\
1&\dots&0&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&\dots&1&0\\
-a^{\prime}_{1}&\dots&-a^{\prime}_{N}&-a^{\prime}_{N+1}\\
a^{\prime}_{1}&\dots&a^{\prime}_{N}&a^{\prime}_{N+1}\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{ with }a^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}d_{1}\\
\vdots\\
d_{m}\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\\
1\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{ and }b^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-1\\[3.0pt]
\frac{1}{N\omega_{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\frac{1}{N\omega_{1}}\\
-\delta_{0}\\
\delta_{0}\end{pmatrix}.

Remark 12
.

Building on the conjugates in Examples
3.2
and
3.3
, we provide full reformulations of the problems in Appendix EC.3. There, we also discuss how our union-ball reformulation substantially simplifies the final form, in comparison with the reformulation given in
Nguyen et al. 2024
, as highlighted in Remark
31
.

3.2
Conditional Risk Functionals in Expectation Form

The decomposition framework naturally extends to a broader class of risk functionals that admit expectation-based representations. In this section, we focus on solving the following problem

\displaystyle\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sup_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Y}\in\bigcup\limits_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}}\mathbb{B}_{\delta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}\right)}\rho_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Y}}\left(Y^{\top}\alpha\right)=\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sup_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}}\left\{\sup_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Y}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}\right)}\rho_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Y}}\left(Y^{\top}\alpha\right)\right\}.

(17)

We begin with risk measures of the form

\displaystyle\rho^{(1)}(Z)=\inf_{t\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}\mathbb{E}\left[\ell(Z,t)\right],

(18)

as considered in
Nguyen et al. 2024
, where only conservative reformulations were available. In contrast, our approach yields reformulations that are not only provably exact, but also substantially simpler–requiring fewer variables, fewer and simpler constraints, and exhibiting a modular structure that facilitates both analysis and computation. The following property of a function is necessary for the propositions in this section.

Definition 3.2
.

(Level-bounded)
A function
\ell:\mathbb{R}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is level bounded if for each
A\in\mathbb{R}
, the sublevel set
\left\{t\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:\ell(t)\leqslant A\right\}
is bounded (possibly empty).

As in the case of conditional expectation, we first exploit regularization equivalents to obtain robust optimization reformulations of (
17
), and then show that these admit convex minimization reformulations.

Proposition 13
.

(i)

Let
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=||y_{1}-y_{2}||_{\mathcal{Y}}
for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
. Suppose that
\ell(z,t):\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is jointly convex in
(z,t)
, level-bounded in
t
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}
, and Lipschitz continuous in
z
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
with a uniform Lipschitz constant
\operatorname{Lip}(\ell)
. The problem (
17
) with
\rho=\rho^{(1)}
is equivalent to the robust optimization problem

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},t\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}\sup_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{+}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\ell(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha,t)+\operatorname{Lip}(\ell)\delta||\alpha||_{*},

and can be solved by the convex program

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},t\in\mathbb{R}^{k},v}\sigma^{*}(v|\mathcal{V}_{+})

\displaystyle~~~~~~\text{s.t.}~v_{i}\geqslant\ell(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha,t),i=1,...,N,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~v_{N+1}\geqslant\operatorname{Lip}(\ell)\|\alpha\|_{*}.

(ii)

Let
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=||y_{1}-y_{2}||_{\mathcal{Y}}^{q}
for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
,
q\in(1,\infty)
and
k=1
. Assume the loss function
\ell
is the form
\ell(z,t):=\left(C\bar{\ell}_{i}(z-t)\right)^{q}
, where
C>0
, and
\bar{\ell}_{i}
is one of the following:

(a)

\bar{\ell}_{1}(x)=\left(\left|x-b_{1}\right|-b_{2}\right)_{+}
with some
b_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
b_{2}\geqslant 0
;

(b)

\bar{\ell}_{2}(x)=\left|x-b_{1}\right|+b_{2}
with some
b_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
b_{2}>0
.

The problem (
17
) with
\rho=\rho^{(1)}
is equivalent to the robust optimization problem

\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},t\in\mathbb{R}}\sup_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{+}}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\ell(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha,t)\right)^{1/q}+C\delta^{\frac{1}{q}}||\alpha||_{*}\right]^{q},

and can be solved by the convex program

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},t\in\mathbb{R},y\geqslant 0,v}\left[\sigma^{*}(v|\mathcal{V}_{+})+C_{q}^{1}y+C_{q}v_{N+1}\left\|\frac{\alpha}{v_{N+1}}\right\|_{*}^{\frac{q}{q-1}}\right]^{q}

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~\text{s.t.}~v_{i}\geqslant\frac{\ell(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha,t)}{y^{q-1}},~i=1,...,N,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~v_{N+1}\geqslant 0,

where
C_{q}^{1}=q^{\frac{1}{1-q}}-q^{\frac{q}{1-q}}
and
C_{q}=C^{\frac{q}{q-1}}\left(q^{\frac{1}{1-q}}-q^{\frac{q}{1-q}}\right)
.

For general convex loss functions
\ell
, we obtain the following convex reformulation by leveraging two key ingredients inherited from the unconditional Wasserstein DRO setting: (i) the projection property, and (ii) the validity of a min–max switching argument, established in the appendix (see Lemma
30
).

Proposition 14
.

Let
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=||y_{1}-y_{2}||_{\mathcal{Y}}^{q}
for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
and
q\in(1,\infty)
. Assume the loss function
\ell
is the form
\ell(z,t)=\sum_{q_{1}\in\mathcal{I}}C_{q_{1}}\left(\bar{\ell}_{q_{1}}(z,t)\right)^{q_{1}}
, with a finite index set
\mathcal{I}\subset[1,q]
and
C_{q_{1}}\geqslant 0
, where
\bar{\ell}_{q_{1}}(z,t):\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is nonnegative, jointly convex in
(z,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
, level-bounded in
t\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}
, and Lipschitz continuous in
z
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
with a uniform Lipschitz constant
\operatorname{Lip}(\bar{\ell}_{q_{1}})
, for
q_{1}\in\mathcal{I}
. The problem (
17
) with
\rho=\rho^{(1)}
can be solved by the convex program

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},t\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\eta\geqslant 0,v}~\sigma^{*}(v|\mathcal{V}_{+})

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~\text{s.t.}~v_{i}\geqslant\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{z\cdot\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha-(\ell(\cdot,t))^{*}(z)+\eta\cdot\frac{|z|^{q^{*}}}{q^{*}q^{q^{*}-1}}\right\},~i=1,...,N,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~v_{N+1}\geqslant||\alpha||_{*}^{q}/\eta^{q-1}.

Remark 15
.

Note that if
\bar{\ell}_{q_{1}}(z,t)=\bar{\ell}_{q_{1}}(z)
,
C_{q_{1}}
can relax the nonnegative constraint, that is,
C_{q_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\bar{\ell}_{q_{1}}(z)
also can relax the level-bounded and convex constraint.

As a demonstrative example, we apply our framework to the mean-variance portfolio allocation problem studied in
Nguyen et al. 2024
, showcasing its ability to yield not only provably exact but also markedly simpler reformulations.

Example 3.4
.

The robustified conditional mean-variance portfolio allocation problem in
Nguyen et al. 2024
is as follows:

\displaystyle\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta_{0}}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}})\\
\mathbb{Q}\left(X\in\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}(x_{0})\right)\in\mathcal{N}_{\omega}\end{subarray}}\operatorname{Variance}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[Y^{\top}\alpha\mid X\in\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right)\right]-\theta\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[Y^{\top}\alpha\mid X\in\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right)\right],

(19)

where
\mathcal{N}_{\omega}=[\varepsilon_{0},1]
,
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1]
and
\theta\geqslant 0
. Suppose in addition that
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}},\mathcal{Y}=\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}},\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,\widehat{x})=\|x-\widehat{x}\|^{2}
and
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y,\widehat{y})=\|y-\widehat{y}\|_{2}^{2}
. The problem (
19
) can be solved by the convex program

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},~0\leqslant\eta\leqslant 1,~t\in\mathbb{R},z,s}~z^{\top}b-\frac{\theta^{2}}{4}-t\theta

\displaystyle~~~~\text{s.t.}~z\geqslant 0,~A^{\top}z\geqslant s

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~s_{i}\geqslant\frac{\left(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha-\theta/2-t\right)^{2}}{1-\eta},~i=1,...,N,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~s_{N+1}\geqslant||\alpha||_{*}^{2}/\eta,~s_{N+2}\geqslant 0,

where
A\in\mathbb{R}^{(N+6)\times(N+2)}
,and
b\in\mathbb{R}^{N+6}
are defined by

\displaystyle A=\begin{pmatrix}1&\dots&1&0&0\\
-1&\dots&-1&0&0\\
0&\dots&0&0&1\\
0&\dots&0&0&-1\\
1&\dots&0&0&-\frac{1}{N}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&\dots&1&0&-\frac{1}{N}\\
-a_{1}&\dots&-a_{N}&-a_{N+1}&-a_{N+2}\\
a_{1}&\dots&a_{N}&a_{N+1}&a_{N+2}\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{ with }a=\begin{pmatrix}-d_{1}\\
\vdots\\
-d_{m}\\
d_{m+1}\\
\vdots\\
d_{N}\\
-1\\[2.15277pt]
\displaystyle\delta_{0}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}d_{i}\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{ and }b=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-1\\[3.0pt]
\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon_{0}}\\
-1\\[3.0pt]
0\\
\vdots\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

Remark 16
.

One can verify that the constraint
s_{i}\geqslant\frac{\left(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha-\theta/2-t\right)^{2}}{1-\eta}
,
i=1,...,N
, can be reformulated as the following second order cone constraints

\displaystyle\left\|\left[\begin{array}[]{c}2\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha-2t-\theta\\
s_{i}-(1-\eta)\end{array}\right]\right\|_{2}\leqslant s_{i}+1+\eta,~i=1,...,N.

and that the constraint
s_{N+1}\geqslant||\alpha||_{*}^{2}/\eta
can be reformulated as the following second order cone constraint

\displaystyle\left\|\left[\begin{array}[]{c}2\alpha\\
\eta-s_{N+1}\end{array}\right]\right\|_{2}\leqslant\eta-s_{N+1}.

Thus, our reformulation is a second-order cone problem (SOCP), while the reformulation of Proposition 4.5 in
Nguyen et al. 2024
is a semi-definite problem (SDP). From this perspective, our formulation has a simpler form and is easy to apply.

Our framework also accommodates other prominent risk measures with expectation-based structure, including those of the form

\displaystyle\rho^{(2)}(Z)=\inf_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{t+\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\ell^{q}(Z,t)\right]\right)^{1/q}\right\},

(20)

for
q\in[1,\infty)
, which lie beyond the scope of both
Nguyen et al. 2024
and
Esteban-Perez and Morales 2022
. The derivation of tractable reformulations for this class proceeds analogously, building on the same decomposition principles introduced earlier.

Proposition 17
.

(i)

Let
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=||y_{1}-y_{2}||_{\mathcal{Y}}
for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
and
q=1
. Suppose that
\ell(z,t):\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is nonnegative, jointly convex in
(z,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and Lipschitz continuous in
z
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
with a uniform Lipschitz constant
\operatorname{Lip}(\ell)
. Moreover, assume
t+\ell(z,t)
is level bounded in
t
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}
. The problem (
17
) with
\rho=\rho^{(2)}
is equivalent to the robust optimization problem

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},t\in\mathbb{R}}\sup_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{+}}t+\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\left[\ell(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha,t)\right]+\operatorname{Lip}(\ell)\delta||\alpha||_{*},

and can be solved by the convex program

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},t\in\mathbb{R},v}t+\sigma^{*}(v|\mathcal{V}_{+})

\displaystyle~~~~~~\text{s.t.}~v_{i}\geqslant\ell(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha,t),i=1,...,N,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~v_{N+1}\geqslant\operatorname{Lip}(\ell)\|\alpha\|_{*}.

(ii)

Let
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=||y_{1}-y_{2}||_{\mathcal{Y}}^{q}
for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
and
q\in(1,\infty)
. Assume the loss function
\ell
is the form
\ell(z,t):=C\bar{\ell}_{i}(z-t)
, where
C>1
, and
\bar{\ell}_{i}
is one of the following:

(a)

\bar{\ell}_{1}(x)=(x-b)_{+}
with some
b\in\mathbb{R}
;

(b)

\bar{\ell}_{2}(x)=\left(\left|x-b_{1}\right|-b_{2}\right)_{+}
with some
b_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
b_{2}\geqslant 0
;

(c)

\bar{\ell}_{3}(x)=\left|x-b_{1}\right|+b_{2}
with some
b_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
b_{2}>0
,

or
\ell(z,t)=C(|z|-t)_{+}
. The problem (
17
) with
\rho=\rho^{(2)}
is equivalent to the robust optimization problem

\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},t\in\mathbb{R}}\sup_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{+}}t+\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\left[\ell^{q}(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha,t)\right]\right)^{1/q}+C\delta^{\frac{1}{q}}||\alpha||_{*},

and can be solved by the convex program

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},t\in\mathbb{R},y\geqslant 0,v}t+\sigma^{*}(v|\mathcal{V}_{+})+C_{q}^{1}y+C_{q}v_{N+1}\left\|\frac{\alpha}{v_{N+1}}\right\|_{*}^{\frac{q}{q-1}}

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~\text{s.t.}~v_{i}\geqslant\ell^{q}(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha,t)/y^{q-1},i=1,...,N,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~v_{N+1}\geqslant 0,

where
C_{q}^{1}=q^{\frac{1}{1-q}}-q^{\frac{q}{1-q}}
and
C_{q}=C^{\frac{q}{q-1}}\left(q^{\frac{1}{1-q}}-q^{\frac{q}{1-q}}\right)
.

For the general case, we again rely on the two key ingredients—the projection property and the min–max switching argument (see Lemma
30
)—to derive tractable convex reformulations.

Proposition 18
.

Let
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=||y_{1}-y_{2}||_{\mathcal{Y}}^{q}
for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
and
q\in(1,\infty)
. Assume that
\ell(z,t)
is nonnegative, jointly convex in
(z,t)\in\mathbb{R^{2}}
, and Lipschitz continuous in
z
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
with a uniform Lipschitz constant
\operatorname{Lip}(\ell)
. Moreover, assume
t+\ell(z,t)
is level bounded in
t
for all
z\in\mathbb{R}
. The problem (
17
) with
\rho=\rho^{(2)}
can be solved by the convex program

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},\eta\geqslant 0,t\in\mathbb{R},v,w\geqslant 0}~t+\sigma^{*}(v|\mathcal{V}_{+})+C_{q}^{1}w

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~\text{s.t.}~v_{i}\geqslant\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{z\cdot\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha-\left(\frac{\ell^{q}(\cdot,t)}{w^{q-1}}\right)^{*}(z)+\eta\cdot\frac{|z|^{q^{*}}}{q^{*}q^{q^{*}-1}}\right\},~i=1,...,N,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~v_{N+1}\geqslant||\alpha||_{*}^{q}/\eta^{q-1}.

Our methodology also applies to risk measures defined implicitly through expectation constraints, such as the utility-based shortfall risk measure, defined by

\displaystyle\rho^{(3)}(Z)=\inf\left\{\kappa\in\mathbb{R}:\mathbb{E}\left[u(-Z-\kappa)\right]\leqslant l\right\},

where
u
is a convex, increasing function and
l
is a fixed constant in the interior of the range of
u
. The same reformulation principles apply.

Proposition 19
.

Let
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=||y_{1}-y_{2}||_{\mathcal{Y}}
for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
and
u:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a convex, increasing and Lipschitz continuous function with Lipschitz constant
\operatorname{Lip}(u)
. The problem (
17
) with
\rho=\rho^{(3)}

is equivalent to the robust optimization problem

\displaystyle\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sup_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{+}}\inf_{\kappa\in\mathbb{R}}\kappa

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~\text{s.t.}\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}u(-\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha-\kappa)+\operatorname{Lip}(u)\delta||\alpha||_{*}\leqslant l

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},\kappa\in\mathbb{R}}\kappa

\displaystyle~~~~~\text{s.t.}\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}u(-\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha-\kappa)+\operatorname{Lip}(u)\delta||\alpha||_{*}\leqslant l~~~\forall(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{+},

and can be solved by the convex program

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},\kappa\in\mathbb{R},v}\kappa

\displaystyle~~~~~~\text{s.t.}~\sigma^{*}(v|\mathcal{V}_{+})\leqslant l,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~v_{i}\geqslant u(-\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha-\kappa),i=1,...,N,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~v_{N+1}\geqslant\operatorname{Lip}(u)||\alpha||_{*}.

Remark 20
.

Note that when
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\mathcal{Y}}^{q}
for some
q\in(1,\infty)
, a convex program reformulation is still possible, but—unlike the earlier cases where several forms of
u
were admissible—it applies only to the linear specification
u(x)=Cx+b
with
C>0
, as required by monotonicity. In this setting, problem (
17
) admits the equivalent convex program:

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},\,\kappa\in\mathbb{R},\,v}\;\kappa

\displaystyle~~~~~~\text{s.t.}~~\sigma^{*}(v\mid\mathcal{V}_{+})+C_{q}v_{N+1}\left\|\tfrac{\alpha}{v_{N+1}}\right\|_{*}^{\tfrac{q}{q-1}}\leqslant l,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~v_{i}\geqslant u(-\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha-\kappa),\quad i=1,\dots,N,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~v_{N+1}\geqslant 0,

where
C_{q}=C^{\tfrac{q}{q-1}}\!\left(q^{\tfrac{1}{1-q}}-q^{\tfrac{q}{1-q}}\right)
.

Finally, we arrive at the following convex reformulation for the general case of a convex utility function
u
for higher order
q
.

Proposition 21
.

Let
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=||y_{1}-y_{2}||_{\mathcal{Y}}^{q}
for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
and
q\in(1,\infty)
. Assuming the function
u
is convex and increasing and satisfies
u(x)-u(x_{0})\leqslant L|x-x_{0}|^{q}+M,x\in\mathbb{R}
for some
L,M>0
, and some
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, the problem (
17
) with
\rho=\rho^{(3)}
can be solved by the convex program

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},\kappa\in\mathbb{R},\eta\geqslant 0,v}\kappa

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~\text{s.t.}~\sigma^{*}(v|\mathcal{V}_{+})\leqslant l,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~v_{i}\geqslant\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{z\cdot\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha-\left(u(\cdot,\kappa)\right)^{*}(z)+\eta\cdot\frac{|z|^{q^{*}}}{q^{*}q^{q^{*}-1}}\right\},~i=1,...,N,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~v_{N+1}\geqslant||\alpha||_{*}^{q}/\eta^{q-1}.

3.3
Conditional Risk Functionals in Distortion Form

We now turn to risk functionals defined through distortion functions, which play a central role in both risk measurement–as distortion risk measures–and decision theory, where they appear as rank-dependent utility functionals. For general distortion functions, these risk measures lack finite-dimensional expectation representations, i.e., (
18
), rendering them fully intractable under prior approaches such as
Nguyen et al. 2024
and
Esteban-Perez and Morales 2022
. A key highlight of our union-ball framework is that, even in this challenging setting, it yields tractable reformulations via regularization equivalents–offering, to our knowledge, the first viable solution framework for distributionally robust conditional distortion problems. We first give the definition of distortion risk measure.

Definition 3.3
.

(distortion risk measure)
Given a random variable
Z
and a distortion function
h:[0,1]\rightarrow[0,1]
, that is,
h
is an increasing function satisfies that
h(0)=0
, and
h(1)=1
, the distortion risk measure is defined as
\rho_{h}(Z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}z\mathrm{~d}h\left(F_{Z}(z)\right)
, where
F_{Z}
is the distribution of
Z
. Specifically, if
h
is a convex function,
\rho_{h}
is a convex, thus coherent, risk measure.

To present our reformulation results, we first introduce the admissible set

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{+}^{h}=\left\{\left(p,\delta,\bar{p}\right):(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{+},\bar{p}\in\mathcal{V}_{p}^{h}\right\},

where

\mathcal{V}_{p}^{h}=\left\{\bar{p}:\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bar{p}_{i}=1,\bar{p}_{i}\geqslant 0,i=1,...,N,h\left(\sum_{i\in J}p_{i}\right)\leqslant\sum_{i\in J}\bar{p}_{i},\forall J\subset\{1,...,N\}\right\},

which is the risk envelope associated with coherent distortion risk measures. Leveraging this set characterization, together with the decomposition principle and regularization equivalents, we obtain the following convex reformulation.

Proposition 22
.

Assume
\rho
is a convex distortion risk measure with convex dstortion function
h
. Denote the left-derivative of
h
on
(0,1]
as
h^{\prime}_{-}
. Then we have following results:

(i)

Let
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=||y_{1}-y_{2}||_{\mathcal{Y}}
for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
and
\ell:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a convex and Lipschitz continuous function.
The problem (
12
) with
\rho=\rho_{h}
is equivalent to the robust optimization problem

\displaystyle\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sup_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{+}}\rho_{\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}}^{h}\left[\ell(Y^{\top}\alpha)\right]+\operatorname{Lip}(\ell)\left\|h_{-}^{\prime}\right\|_{\infty}\delta\|{\alpha}\|_{*}.

(21)

and can be solved by the convex program

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},v}~\sigma^{*}(v|\mathcal{V}_{+}^{h})

\displaystyle~~~\text{s.t.}~v_{i}\geqslant 0,i=1,...,N,~v_{N+1}\geqslant\operatorname{Lip}(\ell)\left\|h_{-}^{\prime}\right\|_{\infty}\|\alpha\|_{*},

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~v_{i+N+1}\geqslant\ell(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha),i=1,...,N.

(ii)

Let
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=||y_{1}-y_{2}||_{\mathcal{Y}}^{q}
for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
and
q\in(1,\infty)
, and
\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{q^{*}}=1
. Assume the function
\ell
takes one of the following two forms, multiplied by a constant
C>0
:

(a)

\ell_{1}(x)=x+b
or
\ell_{1}(x)=-x+b
with some
b\in\mathbb{R}
;

(b)

\ell_{2}(x)=\left|x-b_{1}\right|+b_{2}
with some
b_{1},b_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
.

The problem (
12
) with
\rho=\rho_{h}
is equivalent to the robust optimization problem

\displaystyle\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sup_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{+}}\rho_{\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}}^{h}\left[\ell(Y^{\top}\alpha)\right]+C\left\|h_{-}^{\prime}\right\|_{q^{*}}\delta^{\frac{1}{q}}\|{\alpha}\|_{*}.

and can be solved by the convex program

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},v}\sigma^{*}(v|\mathcal{V}_{+}^{h})+C_{h,q}v_{N+1}\left\|\frac{\alpha}{v_{N+1}}\right\|_{*}^{\frac{q}{q-1}}

\displaystyle~~~\text{s.t.}~v_{i}\geqslant 0,i=1,...,N+1,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~v_{i+N+1}\geqslant\ell(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha),i=1,...,N,

where
C_{h,q}=\left(q^{\frac{1}{1-q}}-q^{\frac{q}{1-q}}\right)\left(C\left\|h_{-}^{\prime}\right\|_{q^{*}}\right)^{\frac{q}{q-1}}
.

Similar to Examples
3.2
and
3.3
, we now apply the admissible sets from Theorems
3
and
6
to illustrate how the conjugate of the support function
\sigma^{*}(v|\mathcal{V}^{h}_{+})
is derived. Recall that
\mathcal{N}_{\omega}=[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]\subset(0,1]
, and
{\varepsilon}=\min_{\beta\in\mathcal{N}_{\omega}}\beta=\omega_{1}
.

Example 3.5
.

Following the definition of
\mathcal{V}
in Theorem
3
, the conjugate function
\sigma^{*}(v|\mathcal{V}_{+}^{h})
is given by

\displaystyle\inf-\frac{\eta_{1}}{\omega_{2}}+\frac{\eta_{2}}{\omega_{1}}-\lambda-\mu+\sum_{j=1}^{2^{N}-2}\zeta_{j}h^{*}(\frac{-\phi_{j}}{\zeta_{j}})

\displaystyle~\text{s.t.}~\lambda,\mu,\xi_{2}\in\mathbb{R},\left\{\phi_{j}\right\}_{j=1}^{2^{N}-2}\in\mathbb{R}^{2^{N}-2},\left\{\zeta_{j}\right\}_{j=1}^{2^{N}-2},\left\{\tau_{i}^{1}\right\}_{i=1}^{N},\left\{\tau_{i}^{2}\right\}_{i=1}^{N},\left\{\gamma_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{N},\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\xi_{1}\geqslant 0

\displaystyle~~~~~~-\xi_{2}(\delta_{0}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}d_{i})+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tau_{i}^{2}+\eta_{1}-\eta_{2}\leqslant 0,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~v_{i}+\lambda+\tau_{i}^{1}-\tau_{i}^{2}+c_{i}+\sum_{j:i\in I_{j}}\phi_{j}\leqslant 0,~i=1,...,N,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~c_{i}=\xi_{2}d_{i},i=1,\ldots,m,~c_{i}=-\xi_{2}d_{i},i=m+1,\ldots,N,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~v_{N+1+i}+\mu+{\sum_{j:i\in I_{j}}\zeta_{j}}+\gamma_{i}\leqslant 0,~i=1,...,N,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~v_{N+1}+\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}\leqslant 0.

Example 3.6
.

Following the definition of
\mathcal{V}
in Theorem
6
, the conjugate function
\sigma^{*}(v|\mathcal{V}_{+}^{h})
is given by

\displaystyle\inf-\lambda-\mu+\frac{1}{N\varepsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tau_{i}^{2}-\xi_{2}\delta_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{2^{N}-2}\zeta_{j}h^{*}(\frac{-\phi_{j}}{\zeta_{j}})

\displaystyle~\text{s.t.}~\lambda,\mu,\xi_{2}\in\mathbb{R},\left\{\phi_{j}\right\}_{j=1}^{2^{N}-2}\in\mathbb{R}^{2^{N}-2},\left\{\zeta_{j}\right\}_{j=1}^{2^{N}-2},\left\{\tau_{i}^{1}\right\}_{i=1}^{N},\left\{\tau_{i}^{2}\right\}_{i=1}^{N},\left\{\gamma_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{N},\xi_{1}\geqslant 0,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~v_{i}+\lambda+\tau_{i}^{1}-\tau_{i}^{2}+c_{i}^{\prime}+\sum_{j:i\in I_{j}}\phi_{j}\leqslant 0,~i=1,...,N,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~c_{i}^{\prime}=\xi_{2}d_{i},i=1,\ldots,m,~c_{i}=0,i=m+1,\ldots,N,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~v_{N+1+i}+\mu+{\sum_{j:i\in I_{j}}\zeta_{j}}+\gamma_{i}\leqslant 0,~i=1,...,N,

\displaystyle~~~~~~~v_{N+1}+\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}\leqslant 0.

The mean-CVaR risk measure considered in the example of
Nguyen et al. 2024
is a convex distortion risk measure. While their approach yields only conservative approximations to the associated portfolio allocation problem, we demonstrate below how our framework provides reformulations that are not only provably exact but also markedly simpler, featuring fewer variables, fewer constraints, and a modular structure that streamlines computation.

Example 3.7
.

The robustified conditional mean-
\mathrm{CVaR}
portfolio allocation problem in
Nguyen et al. 2024
is as follows:

\displaystyle\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta_{0}}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}})\\
\mathbb{Q}\left(X\in\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}(x_{0})\right)\in\mathcal{N}_{\omega}\end{subarray}}\operatorname{CVaR}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{1-\kappa}\left[Y^{\top}\alpha\mid X\in\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right)\right]-\theta\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[Y^{\top}\alpha\mid X\in\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right)\right],

(22)

where
\mathcal{N}_{\omega}=[\varepsilon_{0},1]
,
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1]
,
\theta\geqslant 0
,
\kappa\in(0,1]
,

\operatorname{CVaR}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{1-\kappa}(Z)=\frac{1}{\kappa}\int_{1-\kappa}^{1}\operatorname{VaR}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\phi}(-Z)\operatorname{d}\phi,

and
\operatorname{VaR}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\phi}(Z)=\inf\left\{z\in\mathbb{R}:\mathbb{Q}(Z\leqslant z)\geqslant\phi\right\}
. Suppose in addition that
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}},\mathcal{Y}=\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}},\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{X}}(x,\widehat{x})=\|x-\widehat{x}\|^{2}
and
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y,\widehat{y})=\|y-\widehat{y}\|_{2}^{2}
. The problem (
22
) can be solved by the convex program

\displaystyle\inf_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A},t\in\mathbb{R},z\geqslant 0,v_{N+1},s}~z^{\top}b+s

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~\text{s.t.}~v_{N+1}\geqslant 0,~A^{\top}z\geqslant(\ell(\widehat{y}_{1}^{\top}\alpha,t),\dots,\ell(\widehat{y}_{N}^{\top}\alpha,t),v_{N+1},0)^{\top},

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~s\geqslant C_{\theta,\kappa}v_{N+1}\left\|\frac{\alpha}{v_{N+1}}\right\|_{2}^{2},

where
\ell(z,t)=t+\frac{1}{\kappa}(-z-t)_{+}-\theta z
,
A\in\mathbb{R}^{(N+6)\times(N+2)}
,and
b\in\mathbb{R}^{N+6}
are defined by

\displaystyle A=\begin{pmatrix}1&\dots&1&0&0\\
-1&\dots&-1&0&0\\
0&\dots&0&0&1\\
0&\dots&0&0&-1\\
1&\dots&0&0&-\frac{1}{N}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&\dots&1&0&-\frac{1}{N}\\
-a_{1}&\dots&-a_{N}&-a_{N+1}&-a_{N+2}\\
a_{1}&\dots&a_{N}&a_{N+1}&a_{N+2}\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{ with }a=\begin{pmatrix}-d_{1}\\
\vdots\\
-d_{m}\\
d_{m+1}\\
\vdots\\
d_{N}\\
-1\\[2.15277pt]
\displaystyle\delta_{0}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}d_{i}\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{ and }b=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-1\\[3.0pt]
\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon_{0}}\\
-1\\[3.0pt]
0\\
\vdots\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

Remark 23
.

One can verify that the constraint
s\geqslant C_{\theta,\kappa}v_{N+1}\left\|\frac{\alpha}{v_{N+1}}\right\|_{2}^{2}
can be reformulated as the following SOC constraint

\displaystyle\left\|\left[\begin{array}[]{c}2\alpha\\
s-C_{\theta,\kappa}v_{N+1}\end{array}\right]\right\|_{2}\leqslant s+C_{\theta,\kappa}v_{N+1}.

Thus, our reformulation is a SOCP, while the reformulation of Proposition 4.6 in
Nguyen et al. 2024
is a SDP. From this perspective, our formulation has a simpler form and is easy to apply.

4
Scalable Solution for Large-Scale Distributionally Robust CRM

We now highlight another key advantage of the union-ball decomposition introduced in the previous section: beyond yielding unified and tractable reformulations, it naturally facilitates the development of scalable solution methods. While our earlier convex programs–for instance, those targeting rank-dependent utility–are exact and structurally elegant, they become computationally prohibitive as the sample size
N
grows. In this section, we show how the decomposition structure lends itself to a cutting-plane algorithm, effectively addressing large-scale distributionally robust CRM problems–an area largely unexplored in the existing literature.

We base our development on the distributonally robust conditional rank-dependent utility problem (
21
), which subsumes distributonally robust conditional expectation problem as a special case. Following Proposition
22
,
we have

\displaystyle\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sup_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{+}}\rho_{\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}}^{h}\left[\ell(Y^{\top}\alpha)\right]+\operatorname{Lip}(\ell)\left\|h_{-}^{\prime}\right\|_{\infty}\delta\|{\alpha}\|_{*}

(23)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sup_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{+},\bar{p}\in\mathcal{V}_{p}^{h}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bar{p}_{i}\ell\left(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha\right)+\operatorname{Lip}(\ell)\left\|h_{-}^{\prime}\right\|_{\infty}\delta\|{\alpha}\|_{*}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sup_{(p,\delta,\bar{p})\in\mathcal{V}_{+}^{h}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bar{p}_{i}\ell\left(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha\right)+\operatorname{Lip}(\ell)\left\|h_{-}^{\prime}\right\|_{\infty}\delta\|{\alpha}\|_{*},

(24)

where
\mathcal{V}_{+}^{h}=\left\{(p,\delta,\bar{p}):(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{+},\bar{p}\in\mathcal{V}_{p}^{h}\right\}
.

The computational burden lies in solving the inner supremum problem where the set
\mathcal{V}_{p}^{h}
involves exponentially many constraints as
N
increases. The structure of inner maximization problem of (
24
), which is linear in variable
(\delta,\bar{p})
, for any fixed
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
, naturally leads to the design of a cutting plane method. The central idea is to iteratively refine the uncertainty set by adding only the most violated constraints, allowing convergence without necessarily enumerating all possibilities.

1

Input :
tolerance
\psi_{\mathrm{tol}}>0
; feasible
(p_{f},\delta_{f})\in\mathcal{V}_{+}
; hypothesis set
\mathcal{A}
.

Output :
parameter
{\alpha}^{*}
and an (approximate) worst-case scenario
(p^{*},\delta^{*},\bar{p}^{*})
.

2

Initialization:
Set the scenario pool
\mathcal{U}_{1}\leftarrow\{(p_{f},\delta_{f},\bar{p}_{f})\}
with
\bar{p}_{f}:=p_{f}
; set
j\leftarrow 1
.

3

4

repeat

5

Lower bound with uncertainty set
\mathcal{U}_{j}
:

6

l^{b}_{j}\leftarrow\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\max_{(p,\delta,\bar{p})\in\mathcal{U}_{j}}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bar{p}_{i}\,\ell(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha)+\operatorname{Lip}(\ell)\,\lVert h_{-}^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}\,\delta\,\lVert\alpha\rVert_{*}\right\}

Let
\alpha_{j}
be an optimal solution to obtain the lower bound
l^{b}_{j}
.

7

8

Upper bound with
\alpha_{j}
:

9

Form the order statistics of the realized losses

r_{(1)}\leqslant\dots\leqslant r_{(N)},\qquad r_{i}:=\ell(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha_{j}).

Solve

\displaystyle u^{b}_{j}\leftarrow\sup_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{+}}\;

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}h\!\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}p_{(i)}\right)\,\big(r_{(k)}-r_{(k+1)}\big)+r_{(N)}+\operatorname{Lip}(\ell)\,\lVert h_{-}^{\prime}\rVert_{\infty}\,\delta\,\lVert\alpha_{j}\rVert_{*}.

(25)

Let
(p_{j}^{*},\delta_{j}^{*})
be an optimal solution to obtain the upper bound
u^{b}_{j}
and set

\bar{p}_{j(i)}^{*}:=h\!\left(\sum_{k=1}^{i}p_{j(k)}^{*}\right)-h\!\left(\sum_{k=1}^{i-1}p_{j(k)}^{*}\right),\qquad i=1,\dots,N.

10

Gap check:
If
u^{b}_{j}-l^{b}_{j}\leqslant\psi_{\mathrm{tol}}
, terminate with

\alpha^{*}\leftarrow\alpha_{j}
and
(p^{*},\delta^{*},\bar{p}^{*})\leftarrow(p_{j}^{*},\delta_{j}^{*},\bar{p}_{j}^{*})
.

11

12

Pool augmentation:
Otherwise enlarge the scenario pool

13

\mathcal{U}_{j+1}\leftarrow\mathcal{U}_{j}\cup\{(p_{j}^{*},\delta_{j}^{*},\bar{p}_{j}^{*})\},

and set
j\leftarrow j+1
to continue.

14

until

u^{b}_{j}-l^{b}_{j}\leqslant\psi_{\mathrm{tol}}

15

Algorithm 1

Cutting-Plane Scheme

To efficiently solve (
24
), we proceed as follows:

1.

We begin with an initial single-scenario uncertainty set
\mathcal{U}_{1}=\{(p,\delta,\bar{p})=(p_{f},\delta_{f},{p}_{f})\}
, where
(p_{f},\delta_{f})\in\mathcal{V}_{+}
. The associated scenario
p_{f}
clearly lies within
\mathcal{V}_{p_{f}}^{h}
. Note that we can always find a feasible solution
(p_{f},\delta_{f})\in\mathcal{V}_{+}
when adopting
\mathcal{V}
specified in Theorems
3
and
6
. To guarantee the feasibility of (
23
) with
\mathcal{V}
specified in Theorems
3
and
6
, let
\delta_{0}=\delta_{min}+\Delta
, where
\Delta>0
and
\delta_{min}
is given by Propositions
1
and
4
, respectively. Moreover, let
\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
be the optimal solution such that the optimization problem in Propositions
1
and
4
attain the optimal value
\delta_{min}
, respectively. Then for
\mathcal{V}
specified in Theorem
3
, let
p_{f}=(\frac{v_{1}}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}v_{i}},...,\frac{v_{N}}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}v_{i}})^{\top}
and
\delta_{f}=\frac{N\Delta}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}v_{i}}
and for
\mathcal{V}
specified in Theorem
6
, let
p_{f}=(\frac{v_{1}}{N\varepsilon},...,\frac{v_{N}}{N\varepsilon})^{\top}
and
\delta_{f}=\Delta
. One can verify that
(p_{f},\delta_{f})\in\mathcal{V}_{+}
and thus
(p_{f},\delta_{f},{p}_{f})\in\mathcal{V}_{+}^{h}
. Hence, we can always obtain a feasible scenario in
\mathcal{V}_{+}^{h}
and then
\mathcal{U}_{1}\subset\mathcal{V}_{+}^{h}
.

2.

At iteration
j
, we solve the restricted optimization problem:

\displaystyle\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sup_{(p,\delta,\bar{p})\in\mathcal{U}_{j}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bar{p}_{i}\ell\left(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha\right)+\operatorname{Lip}(\ell)\left\|h_{-}^{\prime}\right\|_{\infty}\delta\|{\alpha}\|_{*}.

Let the optimal solution and optimal value obtained be
(\alpha_{j},l^{b}_{j})
, which provides a lower bound to (
24
).

3.

Fixing
\alpha_{j}
, we solve the full inner maximization of (
24
) to obtain the worst-case solution
(p_{j}^{*},\delta_{j}^{*})
and the corresponding upper bound
u^{b}_{j}
to the problem (
24
). One can verify that given
\alpha_{j}
, we can actually directly solve the inner supremum problem of problem (
23
) because the distortion risk measure has explicit form (see (
25
)) and thereby avoid an exponentially growing number of constraints in (
24
).

4.

We check the convergence criterion by comparing the gap
u^{b}_{j}-l^{b}_{j}
to a predefined tolerance
\psi_{\text{tol}}>0
. If the gap satisfies
u^{b}_{j}-l^{b}_{j}\leqslant\psi_{\text{tol}}
, the algorithm terminates, returning
\alpha_{j}
and
l^{b}_{j}
as approximate optimal solution and optimal value. Otherwise, the scenario
(p_{j}^{*},\delta_{j}^{*},\bar{p}_{j}^{*})
is appended to the uncertainty set, forming
\mathcal{U}_{j+1}=\mathcal{U}_{j}\cup(p_{j}^{*},\delta_{j}^{*},\bar{p}_{j}^{*})
, and the process repeats. A critical observation that significantly enhances efficiency is that, given
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}
, for each worst-case distribution
p^{*}
, the optimal associated distortion-weighted distribution
\bar{p}^{*}
, achieving the supremum, is explicitly computed by:

\bar{p}_{(i)}^{*}=h\left(\sum_{k=1}^{i}p_{(k)}^{*}\right)-h\left(\sum_{k=1}^{i-1}p_{(k)}^{*}\right)

with the
(\cdot)
denoting sorting of scenarios according to
\ell(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha)
in ascending order, i.e.
\ell\left(\widehat{y}_{(1)}^{\top}\alpha\right)\leqslant\ldots\leqslant\ell\left(\widehat{y}_{(N)}^{\top}\alpha\right).
Hence, each iteration only needs to incorporate a single scenario
(p_{j}^{*},\delta_{j}^{*},\bar{p}_{j}^{*})
to the uncertainty set rather than explicitly enumerating exponential constraints,
\{(p_{j}^{*},\delta_{j}^{*},\bar{p}_{j}):\bar{p}_{j}\in\mathcal{V}_{p_{j}^{*}}^{h}\}
, to the uncertainty set.

The fact that
u^{b}_{j}-l^{b}_{j}
converges to
0
as
j
increases is guaranteed by that the lower bound of (
24
),
l^{b}_{j}
, increases with respect to iteration
j
and converges to (
24
) as
j
increases due to
\mathcal{U}_{j}\subset\mathcal{U}_{j+1}\subset...\subset\mathcal{V}_{+}^{h}
.

5
Numerical Experiment

In this section, we demonstrate the practical effectiveness of our unified and tractable optimal transport framework through simulation experiments on a portfolio allocation problem governed by a distributionally robust conditional rank-dependent expected utility (RDEU) criterion. These experiments enable a systematic evaluation of our method in a controlled setting and highlight its advantages over both unconditional models and existing conditional approaches. Leveraging the cutting-plane algorithm developed in the previous section, we solve the resulting problems efficiently and underscore the value of incorporating side information in data-driven decision-making.

We adopt the setting of Proposition
22
(i) for the choice of loss function and distance metric, where the loss function
\ell
is Lipschitz continuous and convex, and the distance metric is defined as
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=\|y_{1}-y_{2}\|_{\mathcal{Y}}
. The distributionally robust conditional model is solved using our union-ball formulation, with the admissible set
\mathcal{V}
specified in Theorem
3
.

We summarize below the models considered in the comparison, each of which is solved using the cutting-plane algorithm.

(i)

Union-Ball Conditional Distributionally Robust Optimization Model (UB-CDRO)
: This is our proposed model, presented as

\displaystyle\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sup_{\mathbb{Q}|_{Y}\in\mathop{\bigcup}\limits_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}}\mathbb{B}_{\delta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}\right)}\rho_{\mathbb{Q}|_{Y}}\left[\ell(Y,\alpha)\right].

(ii)

Unconditional Sample Average Approximation (SAA)
: This baseline model uses the empirical distribution of
Y
without conditioning on
X
-namely,
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}
:

\displaystyle\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\rho_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{N}}^{h}\left[\ell(Y^{\top}\alpha)\right].

(iii)

Unconditional Distributionally Robust Optimization Model (UDRO)
: This model solves the unconditional distributionally robust optimization problem, using the empirical distribution of
Y
as the reference measure. Specifically, this model is presented as

\displaystyle\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sup_{\mathbb{Q}_{Y}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta_{0}}\left(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{N}\right)}\rho_{\mathbb{Q}_{Y}}\left[\ell(Y,\alpha)\right].

(iv)

Conditional Sample Average Approximation (CSAA)
: This model is defined as

\displaystyle\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\rho_{\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{K_{N}}}^{h}\left[\ell(Y^{\top}\alpha)\right],

where
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{K_{N}}
denotes the empirical conditional distribution of
Y
on
X\in\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}(x_{0})
. Specifically,
K_{N}
represents the number of samples whose associated covariates fall within the neighborhood
\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}(x_{0})
. Sorting
(\widehat{x}_{i},\widehat{y}_{i})
as
(\widehat{x}_{(j)},\widehat{y}_{(j)})
such that
\widehat{x}_{(j)}\in\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}(x_{0})
, for
j=1,\dots,K_{N}
and
\widehat{x}_{(j)}\notin\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}(x_{0})
, for
j=K_{N}+1,\dots,N
. Then
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{K_{N}}=\frac{1}{K_{N}}\sum_{j=1}^{K_{N}}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{(j)}}
.

(v)

Conditional Distributionally Robust Optimization Model (CDRO)
: This model solves the distributionally robust counterpart of CSAA, using the empirical conditional distribution
\mathbb{P}_{K_{N}}
as the reference distribution. Specifically, this model is presented as

\displaystyle\min_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\sup_{\mathbb{Q}|_{Y}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta_{0}}\left(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}_{K_{N}}\right)}\rho_{\mathbb{Q}|_{Y}}\left[\ell(Y,\alpha)\right].

In the experiment, we run 50 times for each sample sizes
N
, choose
\delta=\delta_{min}+\frac{1}{10}\frac{\sqrt{ln100}}{N}
to make sure that the model (i) is feasible, and choose
\delta_{0}=100/N
such that the radius
\delta\rightarrow 0
as
N\rightarrow\infty
. For portfolio allocation problem setting,
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{6}_{+}
denotes decision vector and
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{6}
denotes an uncertain quantity of interest with side information
X\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We assume the joint distribution of
(X,Y)
is as follows. The marginal distribution of
X
is
X_{1}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{N}(0.1,10),~X_{2}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and
X_{3}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Let
\mu^{\top}=(86.8625,71.6059,75.3759,97.6258,52.7854,84.8973)
,

L=\begin{bmatrix}136.687&0&0&0&0&0\\
8.79766&142.279&0&0&0&0\\
16.1504&15.0637&122.613&0&0&0\\
18.4944&15.6961&26.344&139.148&0&0\\
3.41394&16.5922&14.8795&13.9914&151.732&0\\
24.8156&18.7292&17.1574&6.36536&24.7703&144.672\end{bmatrix},

which is the same as the setting in Section 5.2 of
Bertsimas and Van 2017
. Moreover, let
\text{gate}(x_{2})=\frac{1}{1+\exp\{-4(x_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2})\}}
,
s\left(x_{2}\right)=\left(1,1-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{gate}\left(x_{2}\right),1+6\operatorname{gate}\left(x_{2}\right),1,1+6\operatorname{gate}\left(x_{2}\right),1\right),

and
D\left(x_{2}\right)=\operatorname{diag}\left(s\left(x_{2}\right)\right)
.
The conditional distribution of
Y
given
X=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
is defined as

Y\mid X=x\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{N}\left(m(x),\Sigma\left(x_{2}\right)\right),

where

m(x)=\mu+x_{1}v_{1}+x_{2}v_{2}+\text{tanh}(x_{3})v_{3},~\text{and }\Sigma\left(x_{2}\right)=LD\left(x_{2}\right)^{2}L^{\top}

with
{v}_{1}=(30,-40,0,15,-10,5)^{\top}
,
{v}_{2}=(-400,60,70,80,90,100)^{\top}
, and
{v}_{3}=(0,20,-25,0,15,-12)^{\top}
. And we choose the conditional set
\mathcal{N}=\{x:\|x-x_{0}\|_{1}\leqslant 1.2\}
, where
x_{0}=(0.1,0.1,0)
.

Figures
1
and
2
report out-of-sample performance for the five models introduced earlier (UB-CDRO, SAA, UDRO, CSAA, CDRO) under various convex distortion functions and Lipschitz-continuous convex loss functions. The plots summarize the distribution of out-of-sample risk values; the shaded regions denote the interquartile range (IQR, 15th-85th percentiles), and the bold colored curves trace the sample means across sample sizes
N
. The figure indicates that for various distortion functions and loss functions, UB-CDRO consistently outperforms all conditional and unconditional benchmarks: its out-of-sample performance is more stable (smaller IQR), exhibits a lower mean, and approaches the oracle optimal value more rapidly as
N
increases. Although the conditional methods (CSAA, CDRO) converge to the conditional optimum, the bands of conditional methods remain wide even at
N=400
, indicating persistent variability. Moreover, the unconditional methods (SAA, UDRO) do not converge to that optimum as
N
grows. This is expected because the conditional distribution can differ substantially from the marginal distribution; consequently, the decision
\alpha
that is optimal for the marginal distribution generally differs from the conditional-optimal decision
\alpha^{*}
. In fact, SAA and UDRO converge to the marginal-optimal decision rather than to
\alpha^{*}
. Thus, unconditional methods (SAA, UDRO) exhibit strong stability but poor convergence, whereas conditional methods (CSAA, CDRO) achieve fast convergence but lack stability. By contrast, UB-CDRO is strong in both stability and convergence, surpassing all conditional and unconditional benchmarks.

h(x)=x^{2}

Supplement: Technical Appendices

EC.1. Regularization Reformulations for Wasserstein DRO
(
Wu et al. 2022
)

In this section, we first review some regularization results in
Wu et al. 2022
, which will be used throughout the proofs of Section 3. Let
L^{q}
be the set of all random variables with a finite
q
th moment For
q\geqslant 1
, and
L^{\infty}
be the set of all bounded random variables, under an arbitrary norm of
\mathcal{Y}
,
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Y}}
. If the cost function on
\mathcal{Y}
is
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=||y_{1}-y_{2}||_{\mathcal{Y}}
for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
, and
\ell
is convex and Lipschitz continuous, the unconditional worst-case risk problem (
14
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\ell(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha)+\operatorname{Lip}(\ell)\delta||\alpha||_{*},

(26)

where
||\cdot||_{*}
is the dual norm of
||\cdot||_{\mathcal{Y}}
, and
\operatorname{Lip}(\ell)
is the Lipschitz constant of
\ell
. Moreover, for
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=||y_{1}-y_{2}||_{\mathcal{Y}}^{q}
for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
, with
q\in(1,\infty]
,
Wu et al. 2022
have some regularization results for the unconditional worst-case risk problem (
14
) with some special loss functions.

Theorem 24
.

(
Wu et al. 2022
Theorem 3)
Let
\ell:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a convex function. For
q\in(1,\infty]
, suppose that
\mathbb{E}[|\ell(Z)|]<\infty
for all
Z\in L^{q}
, and
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=||y_{1}-y_{2}||_{\mathcal{Y}}^{q}
, for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
. Then the following statements are equivalent.

(i)

There exists
C>0
such that for any
(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}
with
\delta\geqslant 0
, it holds that

\sup_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Y}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}})}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Y}}\left[\ell(Y^{\top}\alpha)\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\ell(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha)+C\delta^{\frac{1}{q}}||\alpha||_{*}

(ii)

The function
\ell
takes one of the following two forms, multiplied by
C
:

(a)

\ell_{1}(x)=x+b
or
\ell_{1}(x)=-x+b
with some
b\in\mathbb{R}
;

(b)

\ell_{2}(x)=\left|x-b_{1}\right|+b_{2}
with some
b_{1},b_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
.

Theorem 25
.

(
Wu et al. 2022
Theorem 4)
Let
\ell:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
be a Lipschitz continuous and convex function. For any
q\in(1,\infty)
, assume
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=||y_{1}-y_{2}||_{\mathcal{Y}}^{q}
, for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
. Then the following statements are equivalent.

(i)

There exists
C>0
such that for any
(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}
with
\delta\geqslant 0
, it holds that

\sup_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Y}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}})}\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Y}}\left[\ell^{q}(Y^{\top}\alpha)\right]=\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\ell^{q}(\widehat{y}_{i}^{\top}\alpha)\right)^{1/q}+C\delta^{\frac{1}{q}}||\alpha||_{*}\right]^{q}.

(ii)

The function
\ell
takes one of the following four forms, multiplied by
C
:

(a)

\ell_{1}(x)=(x-b)_{+}
with some
b\in\mathbb{R}
;

(b)

\ell_{2}(x)=(x-b)_{-}
with some
b\in\mathbb{R}
;

(c)

\ell_{3}(x)=\left(\left|x-b_{1}\right|-b_{2}\right)_{+}
with some
b_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
b_{2}\geqslant 0
;

(d)

\ell_{4}(x)=\left|x-b_{1}\right|+b_{2}
with some
b_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
b_{2}>0
.

Theorem 26
.

(
Wu et al. 2022
Corollary 1)
For any
q\in[1,\infty)
and
C>0
, let
\rho^{(1)}
be defined by (
18
) with
\ell(z,t)=(C\bar{\ell})^{q}(z,t)
and
\bar{\ell}(z,t)=C\bar{\ell}(z-t)
, and
\rho^{(2)}
be defined by (
20
) with
{\ell}(z,t)={\ell}(z-t)
, and assume
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=||y_{1}-y_{2}||_{\mathcal{Y}}^{q}
, for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
. Take
(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}
with
\delta\geqslant 0
.

(i)

Let the function
\bar{\ell}
take one of the following two forms:

(a)

\bar{\ell}_{1}(x)=\left(\left|x-b_{1}\right|-b_{2}\right)_{+}
with some
b_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
b_{2}\geqslant 0
;

(b)

\bar{\ell}_{2}(x)=\left|x-b_{1}\right|+b_{2}
with some
b_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
b_{2}>0
.

Then

\sup_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Y}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}})}\rho^{(1)}_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Y}}\left(Y^{\top}\alpha\right)=\left[\left(\rho^{(1)}_{\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}}\left(Y^{\top}\alpha\right)\right)^{1/q}+C\delta^{1/q}||\alpha||_{*}\right]^{q}.

(ii)

For
C>1
, let the function
\ell
take one of the following three forms:

(a)

\ell_{1}(x)=(x-b)_{+}
with some
b\in\mathbb{R}
;

(b)

\ell_{2}(x)=\left(\left|x-b_{1}\right|-b_{2}\right)_{+}
with some
b_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
b_{2}\geqslant 0
;

(c)

\ell_{3}(x)=\left|x-b_{1}\right|+b_{2}
with some
b_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
b_{2}>0
,

or
\ell(z,t)=C(|z|-t)_{+}
. It holds that

\sup_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Y}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}})}\rho^{(2)}_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Y}}\left(Y^{\top}\alpha\right)=\rho^{(2)}_{\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}}\left(Y^{\top}\alpha\right)+C\delta^{1/q}||\alpha||_{*}.

Theorem 27
.

(
Wu et al. 2022
Theorem 5)
For
q\in[1,\infty]
, let
h:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be an increasing and convex distortion function satisfying
\lim_{x\rightarrow 1-}h(x)=h(1)
and
\left\|h_{-}^{\prime}\right\|_{q^{*}}\in(0,\infty)
, let
\ell:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a convex function, and let
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(y_{1},y_{2})=||y_{1}-y_{2}||_{\mathcal{Y}}^{q}
, for any
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathcal{Y}
.

(i)

Let
q=1
. If
\operatorname{Lip}(\ell)<\infty
, then for any
(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}
with
\delta\geqslant 0
, we have

\sup_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Y}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}})}\rho_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Y}}^{h}\left(\ell\left(Y^{\top}\alpha\right)\right)=\rho_{\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}}^{h}\left(\ell\left(Y^{\top}\alpha\right)\right)+\operatorname{Lip}(\ell)\left\|h_{-}^{\prime}\right\|_{\infty}\delta\|\alpha\|_{*}.

(ii)

Let
q\in(1,\infty]
. If
\rho_{h}(|\ell(Z)|)<\infty
for all
Z\in L^{q}
, then the following statements are equivalent.

(1)

For any
(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}
with
\delta\geqslant 0
, there exists
C>0
such that

\sup_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Y}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta}(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}})}\rho_{{\mathbb{Q}}|_{Y}}^{h}\left(\ell\left(Y^{\top}\alpha\right)\right)=\rho_{\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}}^{h}\left(\ell\left(Y^{\top}\alpha\right)\right)+C\left\|h_{-}^{\prime}\right\|_{q^{*}}\delta^{1/q}\|\alpha\|_{*}.

(2)

The function
\ell
takes one of the following two forms, multiplied by
C
:

(a)

\ell_{1}(x)=x+b
or
\ell_{1}(x)=-x+b
with some
b\in\mathbb{R}
;

(b)

\ell_{2}(x)=\left|x-b_{1}\right|+b_{2}
with some
b_{1},b_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
.

EC.2. Main Proofs

EC.2.1. Proofs of Section
2

Proof of Proposition
1
.

To obtain the sufficient and necessary feasible condition of problem (
7
), we only need to find the sufficient and necessary feasible condition of problem (
8
) and thus only need to find the sufficient and necessary condition such that the ambiguity set
\mathcal{K}({\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right),\mathcal{N}_{\omega},\mathbb{B}_{\delta_{0}}}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}))
is not empty. We consider the following three cases: (i)
\frac{N-m}{N}>\omega_{2}
; (ii)
\frac{N-m}{N}\in[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]=\mathcal{N}_{\omega}
; and (iii)
\frac{N-m}{N}<\omega_{1}
. Before the discussion case by case, we first note that

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}({\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right),\mathcal{N}_{\omega},\mathbb{B}_{\delta_{0}}}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}))=\left(\mathop{\bigcup}\limits_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{1}}\mathbb{B}_{\delta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}\right)\right)\mathop{\bigcup}\left(\mathop{\bigcup}\limits_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{2}}\mathbb{B}_{\delta}^{\circ}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}\right)\right):=\mathcal{U_{B}}

from the proof of Theorem
3
, where
\mathcal{V}_{1}=\{(p,\delta):\exists\varepsilon\text{~such that }(p,\delta,\varepsilon)\in\mathcal{V}_{0},p_{i}=\frac{\varepsilon}{N},\text{ for }i=m+1,..,N\}
and
\mathcal{V}_{2}=\{(p,\delta):\exists\varepsilon\text{~such that }(p,\delta,\varepsilon)\in\mathcal{V}_{0},\exists i\in\{m+1,..,N\}\text{~such that }p_{i}<\frac{\varepsilon}{N}\}
. Thus,
\mathcal{K}({\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right),\mathcal{N}_{\omega},\mathbb{B}_{\delta_{0}}}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}))
is not empty is equivalent to
\mathcal{U_{B}}
is not empty.

(i)

For
\frac{N-m}{N}>\omega_{2}
, one can verify that
\mathcal{V}_{1}=\emptyset
due to that for every
(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{1}
, we have
\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}p_{i}=\frac{N-m}{N}\varepsilon>1
, which is contradictory to the constraint
\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}=1
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}({\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right),\mathcal{N}_{\omega},\mathbb{B}_{\delta_{0}}}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}))=\mathop{\bigcup}\limits_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{2}}\mathbb{B}_{\delta}^{\circ}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}\right)=\mathop{\bigcup}\limits_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}}\mathbb{B}_{\delta}^{\circ}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}\right),

where the second equality follows from that
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}_{1}\cup\mathcal{V}_{2}
and
\mathcal{V}_{1}=\emptyset
. Thus,
\mathcal{K}({\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right),\mathcal{N}_{\omega},\mathbb{B}_{\delta_{0}}}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}))
is not empty if and only if there exists
(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}
such that
\delta>0
, and thus if and only if there exists
(p,\delta,\varepsilon)\in\mathcal{V}_{0}
such that
\delta=\varepsilon\left(\delta_{0}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}d_{i}\right)-\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}d_{i}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}p_{i}d_{i}>0
. One can verify that there exists
(p,\delta,\varepsilon)\in\mathcal{V}_{0}
such that
\delta>0
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{i}\in\left[0,\frac{\varepsilon}{N}\right],i=1,...,N,\\
\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}=1,\varepsilon\in[\frac{1}{\omega_{2}},\frac{1}{\omega_{1}}]\end{subarray}}\varepsilon\left(\delta_{0}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}d_{i}\right)-\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}d_{i}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}p_{i}d_{i}>0,

and thus equivalent to

\displaystyle\delta_{0}>

\displaystyle\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{i}\in\left[0,\frac{\varepsilon}{N}\right],i=1,...,N,\\
\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}=1,\varepsilon\in[\frac{1}{\omega_{2}},\frac{1}{\omega_{1}}]\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{N}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{Np_{i}}{\varepsilon}d_{i}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}\left(1-\frac{Np_{i}}{\varepsilon}\right)d_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}v_{i}\in[0,1],i=1,...,N,\\
1/N\sum_{i=1}^{N}v_{i}\in[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{N}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}v_{i}d_{i}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}(1-v_{i})d_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{min}.

We conclude that in this case problem (
7
) is feasible if and only if
\delta_{0}>\delta_{min}
.

(ii)

For
\frac{N-m}{N}\in[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]=\mathcal{N}_{\omega}
, we find that
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}\left(X\in\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right)\right)\in[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]=\mathcal{N}_{\omega}
and then
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}|_{Y}\in\mathcal{K}({\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right),\mathcal{N}_{\omega},\mathbb{B}_{\delta_{0}}}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}))
. Thus,
\mathcal{K}({\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right),\mathcal{N}_{\omega},\mathbb{B}_{\delta_{0}}}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}))
is not empty if and only if
\delta_{0}\geqslant 0
. Moreover,
\min_{v_{i}\in[0,1],i=1,...,N}\frac{1}{N}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}v_{i}d_{i}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}(1-v_{i})d_{i}\right)\geqslant 0
and can attain lower bound
0
in
v_{i}=0
for
i=1,...,m
, and
v_{i}=1
for
i=m+1,...,N
, and we find that
\sum_{i=1}^{N}v_{i}=\frac{N-m}{N}\in[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]
. Thus,

\delta_{min}=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}v_{i}\in[0,1],i=1,...,N,\\
1/N\sum_{i=1}^{N}v_{i}\in[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{N}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}v_{i}d_{i}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}(1-v_{i})d_{i}\right)=0.

From above discussion, we conclude that in this case problem (
7
) is feasible if and only if
\delta_{0}\geqslant\delta_{min}
.

(iii)

For
\frac{N-m}{N}<\omega_{1}
, if
\delta_{0}\geqslant\delta_{min}
, we consider in the two cases: (iiia)
\delta_{0}>\delta_{min}
, and (iiib)
\delta_{0}=\delta_{min}
.

(iiia)

If
\delta_{0}>\delta_{min}
, there exist
\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
satisfies that
v_{i}\in[0,1],i=1,...,N,1/N\sum_{i=1}^{N}v_{i}\in[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]
such that
\delta_{0}>\frac{1}{N}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}v_{i}d_{i}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}(1-v_{i})d_{i}\right)
. Given
\varepsilon\in[\frac{1}{\omega_{2}},\frac{1}{\omega_{1}}]
, let
p_{i}=\frac{\varepsilon v_{i}}{N}\in[0,\frac{\varepsilon}{N}]
, and
\delta=\varepsilon\left(\delta_{0}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}d_{i}\right)-\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}d_{i}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}p_{i}d_{i}
. Then

\delta=\varepsilon\left[\delta_{0}-\frac{1}{N}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}v_{i}d_{i}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}(1-v_{i})d_{i}\right)\right]>0,

and thus there exists
(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}
such that
\delta>0
. As a result, there exists
(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}
such that
\mathbb{B}_{\delta}^{\circ}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}\right)
is not empty. From the construction of
\mathcal{K}({\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right),\mathcal{N}_{\omega},\mathbb{B}_{\delta_{0}}}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}))
, we conclude that
\mathcal{K}({\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right),\mathcal{N}_{\omega},\mathbb{B}_{\delta_{0}}}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}))
is not empty.

(iiib)

If
\delta_{0}=\delta_{min}
, from the definition of
\delta_{min}
, we find that there exist
\{v_{i}^{*}\}_{i=1}^{N}
satisfies that
v_{i}^{*}\in[0,1],i=1,...,N,1/N\sum_{i=1}^{N}v_{i}^{*}\in[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]
such that

\delta_{0}=\delta_{min}=\frac{1}{N}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}v_{i}^{*}d_{i}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}(1-v_{i}^{*})d_{i}\right)

due to that
\delta_{min}
is a linear problem over a closed and convex set. We claim that
\{v_{i}^{*}\}_{i=1}^{N}
must satisfy that
v_{i}^{*}=1
for
i=m+1,..,N
. Otherwise, without loss of generality, we assume
v_{i}^{*}=1
for
i=m+1,...,k
and
v_{i}^{*}<1
for
i=k+1,...,N
. Let
v_{i}=v_{i}^{*}-\Delta_{i}\geqslant 0
for
i=1,..,m
and
v_{i}=1
for
i=m+1,...,N
, where
\Delta_{i}\geqslant 0
and
\sum_{i=1}^{m}\Delta_{i}=\sum_{i=k+1}^{N}(1-v_{i}^{*})
. We find
\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
satisfies that
v_{i}\in[0,1],i=1,...,N,1/N\sum_{i=1}^{N}v_{i}\in[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]
and such
\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
must exists due to that
\frac{N-m}{N}<\omega_{1}\leqslant\omega_{2}
. Thus we have
\sum_{i=1}^{m}v_{i}d_{i}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}(1-v_{i})d_{i}<\sum_{i=1}^{m}v_{i}^{*}d_{i}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}(1-v_{i}^{*})d_{i}=\delta_{min}
and the strict inequality contradicts the definition of
\delta_{min}
. As a result, the claim holds. Given
\varepsilon\in[\frac{1}{\omega_{2}},\frac{1}{\omega_{1}}]
, using the same construction on (iiia), we find that there exists
(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}
such that
\delta=0
with
p_{i}=\frac{\varepsilon}{N}
for
i=m+1,...,N
. Thus,
(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{1}
. As a result, there exists
(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{1}
such that
\mathbb{B}_{\delta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}\right)=\{\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}\}
is not empty. From the construction of
\mathcal{K}({\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right),\mathcal{N}_{\omega},\mathbb{B}_{\delta_{0}}}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}))
, we conclude that
\mathcal{K}({\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right),\mathcal{N}_{\omega},\mathbb{B}_{\delta_{0}}}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}))
is not empty.

Combining the above two cases, we find that if
\delta_{0}\geqslant\delta_{min}
,
\mathcal{K}({\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right),\mathcal{N}_{\omega},\mathbb{B}_{\delta_{0}}}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}))
is not empty, and thus problem (
7
) is feasible. For the “only if” part, if
\delta_{0}<\delta_{min}
, one can verify that for any
(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}
,

\displaystyle\delta=

\displaystyle\varepsilon\left(\delta_{0}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}d_{i}\right)-\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}d_{i}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}p_{i}d_{i}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\varepsilon\left(\delta_{min}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}d_{i}\right)-\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}d_{i}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}p_{i}d_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}v_{i}\in[0,1],i=1,...,N,\\
1/N\sum_{i=1}^{N}v_{i}\in[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]\end{subarray}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(\frac{\varepsilon v_{i}}{N}-p_{i}\right)d_{i}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}\left(p-\frac{\varepsilon v_{i}}{N}\right)d_{i}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle 0,

where the last inequality follows from that there exists
\{v_{i}^{0}\}_{i=1}^{N}=\{Np_{i}/\varepsilon\}_{i=1}^{N}
satisfies
v_{i}^{0}\in[0,1],i=1,...,N,
and
1/N\sum_{i=1}^{N}v_{i}^{0}\in[\omega_{1},\omega_{2}]
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(\frac{\varepsilon v_{i}^{0}}{N}-p_{i}\right)d_{i}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}\left(p-\frac{\varepsilon v_{i}^{0}}{N}\right)d_{i}=0
. From the construction of
\mathcal{K}({\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right),\mathcal{N}_{\omega},\mathbb{B}_{\delta_{0}}}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}))
, we conclude that
\mathcal{K}({\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right),\mathcal{N}_{\omega},\mathbb{B}_{\delta_{0}}}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}))
is empty. From above discussion, we conclude that in this case problem (
7
) is feasible if and only if
\delta_{0}\geqslant\delta_{min}
.

∎

Proof of Theorem
3
.

Let
\mathcal{V}_{1}=\{(p,\delta):\exists\varepsilon\text{~such that }(p,\delta,\varepsilon)\in\mathcal{V}_{0},p_{i}=\frac{\varepsilon}{N},\text{ for }i=m+1,..,N\}
and
\mathcal{V}_{2}=\{(p,\delta):\exists\varepsilon\text{~such that }(p,\delta,\varepsilon)\in\mathcal{V}_{0},\exists i\in\{m+1,..,N\}\text{~such that }p_{i}<\frac{\varepsilon}{N}\}
. Then
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}_{1}\cup\mathcal{V}_{2}
. We first prove that

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}({\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right),\mathcal{N}_{\omega},\mathbb{B}_{\delta_{0}}}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}))=\left(\mathop{\bigcup}\limits_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{1}}\mathbb{B}_{\delta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}\right)\right)\mathop{\bigcup}\left(\mathop{\bigcup}\limits_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{2}}\mathbb{B}_{\delta}^{\circ}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}\right)\right),

where
\mathbb{B}_{\delta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}\right)
is the ambiguity set on
\mathcal{Y}
, defined as

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{\delta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}\right)=\left\{\mathbb{Q}|_{Y}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{Y}):\mathcal{W}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}},\mathbb{Q}|_{Y}\right)\leqslant\delta\right\},

and
\mathbb{B}_{\delta}^{\circ}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}\right)
is its interior point set, defined as

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{\delta}^{\circ}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}\right)=\left\{\mathbb{Q}|_{Y}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{Y}):\mathcal{W}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}},\mathbb{Q}|_{Y}\right)<\delta\right\}.

To obtain the set equivalence, we first prove the following containment relation

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}({\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right),\mathcal{N}_{\omega},\mathbb{B}_{\delta_{0}}}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}))\subset\left(\mathop{\bigcup}\limits_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{1}}\mathbb{B}_{\delta}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}\right)\right)\mathop{\bigcup}\left(\mathop{\bigcup}\limits_{(p,\delta)\in\mathcal{V}_{2}}\mathbb{B}_{\delta}^{\circ}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}\right)\right).

For any
\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta_{0}}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}),\mathbb{Q}\left(X\in\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right)\right)\in\mathcal{N}_{\omega}
, assume that the optimal transport between
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}
and
\mathbb{Q}
is
\pi^{*}
. Then
\mathbb{Q}
have the following representation by support set and
\pi^{*}
,

\mathbb{Q}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathbb{Q}_{i}^{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(\frac{1}{N}-p_{i}\right)\mathbb{Q}_{i}^{2},

where
\text{supp }\mathbb{Q}_{i}^{1}\subset\{(x,y):x\in\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right)\}
,
\text{supp }\mathbb{Q}_{i}^{2}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\backslash\{(x,y):x\in\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right)\}
,
\mathbb{Q}_{i}^{1}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{i}^{2}
are transferred from
\mathrm{I}_{(\widehat{x}_{i},\widehat{y}_{i})}
under
\pi^{*}
with probability
p_{i}
and
\frac{1}{N}-p_{i}
, respectively,
p_{i}\in[0,\frac{1}{N}]
and
\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\in\mathcal{N}_{\omega}
. Based on this decomposition of
\mathbb{Q}
, if the joint distribution of
(X,Y)
is
\mathbb{Q}
, the conditional distribution of
Y
given
X\in\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}(x_{0})
,
\mathbb{Q}|_{Y}
, can be written as
\mathbb{Q}|_{Y}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\mathbb{Q}_{i}^{1}|_{Y}}{\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}}
. Note that
\text{supp }\mathbb{Q}_{i}^{2}\subset\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\backslash\{(x,y):x\in\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right)\}
,
(\widehat{x}_{i},\widehat{y}_{i})\in\{(x,y):x\in\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right)\}
,
i=m+1,...,N
, and
\widehat{x}_{i}^{p}=\arg\min_{x\in\partial\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right)}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{X}}\left(x,\widehat{x}_{i}\right)
. Then
\int\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{X}}(\widehat{x}_{i},x)~d\mathbb{Q}_{i}^{2}(x,y)>d_{i}
for
i=m+1,...,N
. Due to the continuity of
\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{X}}
, there exists
\phi_{\mathbb{Q}}^{i}>0
and
\psi_{\mathbb{Q}}^{i}>0,~i=m+1,...,N
such that
\int\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{X}}(\widehat{x}_{i},x)~d\mathbb{Q}_{i}^{2}(x,y)\geqslant\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{X}}(\widehat{x}_{i},\widehat{x}_{i}^{p}+\phi^{i}_{\mathbb{Q}}{(\widehat{x}_{i}^{p}-\widehat{x}_{i})})=d_{i}+\psi^{i}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Let
\psi_{\mathbb{Q}}=\min_{i=m+1,...,N}\psi_{\mathbb{Q}}^{i}
. One can verify that under the optimal transport
\pi^{*}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}},\mathbb{Q})=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\int\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{X}}(\widehat{x}_{i},x)+\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\widehat{y}_{i},y)~d\mathbb{Q}_{i}^{1}(x,y)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{m}(\frac{1}{N}-p_{i})\int\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{X}}(\widehat{x}_{i},x)+\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\widehat{y}_{i},y)~d\mathbb{Q}_{i}^{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}p_{i}\int\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{X}}(\widehat{x}_{i},x)+\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\widehat{y}_{i},y)~d\mathbb{Q}_{i}^{1}(x,y)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}(\frac{1}{N}-p_{i})\int\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{X}}(\widehat{x}_{i},x)+\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\widehat{y}_{i},y)~d\mathbb{Q}_{i}^{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\int d_{i}+\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\widehat{y}_{i},y)~d\mathbb{Q}_{i}^{1}(x,y)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{m}(\frac{1}{N}-p_{i})\int\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{X}}(\widehat{x}_{i},\widehat{x}_{i})+\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\widehat{y}_{i},\widehat{y}_{i})~d\mathbb{Q}_{i}^{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}p_{i}\int\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{X}}(\widehat{x}_{i},\widehat{x}_{i})+\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\widehat{y}_{i},y)~d\mathbb{Q}_{i}^{1}(x,y)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}(\frac{1}{N}-p_{i})\int\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{X}}(\widehat{x}_{i},\widehat{x}_{i}^{p}+\phi_{\mathbb{Q}}^{i}{(\widehat{x}_{i}^{p}-\widehat{x}_{i})})+\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\widehat{y}_{i},\widehat{y}_{i})~d\mathbb{Q}_{i}^{2}(x,y).

Rearranging and combining the equations, and applying the definition of the optimal transport, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}},\mathbb{Q})\geqslant

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}\int\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{Y}}(\widehat{y}_{i},y)~d\mathbb{Q}_{i}^{1}|_{Y}(y)+\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}d_{i}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}\left(\frac{1}{N}-p_{i}\right)d_{i}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}\left(\frac{1}{N}-p_{i}\right)\psi_{\mathbb{Q}}

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N}p_{j}\right)\mathcal{W}(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{p_{i}}{\sum_{j=1}^{N}p_{j}}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}},\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{p_{i}}{\sum_{j=1}^{N}p_{j}}\mathbb{Q}_{i}^{1}|_{Y})

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}d_{i}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}\left(\frac{1}{N}-p_{i}\right)d_{i}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}\left(\frac{1}{N}-p_{i}\right)\psi_{\mathbb{Q}}.

(27)

Denote
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{N}p_{j}}
, and
p_{i}^{\prime}=\frac{p_{i}}{\sum_{j=1}^{N}p_{j}}
,
i=1,...,N
. Then
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{N}_{\omega}
,
p_{i}^{\prime}\in[0,\frac{\varepsilon}{N}]
,
i=1,...,N
,
\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}^{\prime}=1
, and
\mathbb{Q}|_{Y}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}^{\prime}\mathbb{Q}_{i}^{1}|_{Y}
. Let
\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{*}|_{Y}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}^{\prime}\mathrm{I}_{\widehat{y}_{i}}.
Then inequality (
Proof of Theorem
.
) can be rearranged as

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{*}|_{Y},\mathbb{Q}|_{Y})\leqslant

\displaystyle\varepsilon\mathcal{W}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}},\mathbb{Q})-\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}^{\prime}d_{i}-\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{N}-p_{i}^{\prime}\right)d_{i}-\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{N}-p_{i}^{\prime}\right)\psi_{\mathbb{Q}}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\varepsilon\delta_{0}-\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}^{\prime}d_{i}-\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{N}-p_{i}^{\prime}\right)d_{i}-\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{N}-p_{i}^{\prime}\right)\psi_{\mathbb{Q}}.

(28)

We consider in the following two cases.

(i)

If
p_{i}^{\prime}=\frac{\varepsilon}{N}
for
i=m+1,...,N
, from inequality (
Proof of Theorem
.
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{*}|_{Y},\mathbb{Q}|_{Y})\leqslant

\displaystyle\varepsilon\delta_{0}-\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}^{\prime}d_{i}-\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{N}-p_{i}^{\prime}\right)d_{i}-\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{N}-p_{i}^{\prime}\right)\psi_{\mathbb{Q}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon\delta_{0}-\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}^{\prime}d_{i}=\delta.

(29)

(ii)

If there exists
i\in\{m+1,...,N\}
such that
p_{i}^{\prime}<\frac{\varepsilon}{N}
, from inequality (
Proof of Theorem
.
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}^{*}|_{Y},\mathbb{Q}|_{Y})\leqslant

\displaystyle\varepsilon\delta_{0}-\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}^{\prime}d_{i}-\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{N}-p_{i}^{\prime}\right)d_{i}-\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{N}-p_{i}^{\prime}\right)\psi_{\mathbb{Q}}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\varepsilon\delta_{0}-\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}^{\prime}d_{i}-\sum_{i=m+1}^{N}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{N}-p_{i}^{\prime}\right)d_{i}=\delta.

(30)

We conclude that, for any
\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{B}_{\delta_{0}}(\widehat{\mathbb{P}}),\mathbb{Q}\left(X\in\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right)\right)\in\mathcal{N}_{\omega}
, the conditional distribution of
Y
given
X\in\mathcal{N}_{\gamma}\left(x_{0}\right)
,
\mathbb{Q}|_{Y}
, satisfies the inequality (
(i)
) or inequality (
(ii)
), where
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{N}_{\omega}
,
p_{i}^{\prime}\in[0,\frac{\varepsilon}{N}]
,
i=1,...,N
,
\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}^{\prime}=1
. Thus, we have
